Thus the original sum is, up to $O(xL^{-D_*-1})$, an integral and a sum of total variation at most $L^{C_{f}}$ of expressions <a id="eq:typeii-separated-B"></a>$$\label{eq:typeii-separated-B}
 B_0=\sum_{\substack{EL^{-C_s}\leq e\leq E\\n\asymp V}}
 a_e(e)\beta_n
       \sum_{\substack{m\asymp U,\ h\geq1\\mn=2eh+1}}
                     \alpha_m a_h(h).$$ The exponent $C_f$ and every coefficient exponent in the next display are independent of $K_0$: <a id="eq:typeii-separated-coefficients"></a>$$\label{eq:typeii-separated-coefficients}
\begin{gathered}
 \lvert a_e(e)\rvert\leq L^{C_{a}},\qquad
 a_h(h)=h^{i\varrho}W_\ell(h)H(h_*),\\
 H(t)=\sum_{pr=t}
     \mathbf 1_{p\in I_{**},\,P^{-}(p)>W}p^{i\sigma}c_r,
 \quad \lvert c_r\rvert\leq L^{C_{a}}.
\end{gathered}$$ In particular $|a_h(h)|\leq L^{O(1)}\tau(h_*)$. To verify these statements, only group-free prime slots go into $e$, so $(eh)_*=eh_*$ and $W_\ell(eh)=W_\ell(h)$. For a fixed pattern, the number of ordered pure-prime lists at a fixed product is at most $(r_0!)^{j_x+1}=L^{O(1)}$. This proves both the $a_e$ bound and the bound for the residual coefficient $c_r$. Fixing the exceptional integer as a divisor gives the bound for $a_h$. Individual slot twists have modulus one; the common power of $h$ from the cutoff is $h^{i\varrho}$. The frequencies $\varrho,\sigma$ have fixed power bounds in $L$.

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### Cauchy’s inequality and the diagonal

By Cauchy’s inequality in $(e,n)$, satisfies <a id="eq:typeii-cauchy"></a>$$\label{eq:typeii-cauchy}
 |B_0|^2\leq EVL^{C_3}\mathcal N,
 \qquad
 \mathcal N=\sum_{e,n}\eta_e(e/E)\eta_n(n/V)
 \left|\sum_{\substack{m\asymp U,\ h\geq1\\mn=2eh+1}}
                           \alpha_ma_h(h)\right|^2.$$ Here $\eta_e,\eta_n$ are nonnegative smooth majorants, bounded by a fixed constant, equal to one on the original ranges. They vanish unless $$\tfrac12 EL^{-C_s}\leq e\leq2E,\qquad n\asymp V.$$ Their derivatives of order $j$ in their displayed arguments are $O_j(L^{C_4(j+1)})$, with $C_3,C_4$ independent of $K_0$. For instance the lower edge of $\eta_e$ is obtained by rescaling a fixed smooth cutoff by $L^{C_s}$.

On the diagonal $m=m'$ the two equations force $h=h'$. For fixed $m,n$, the allowable $e$ divide $(mn-1)/2$, and $$\sum_{e\mid(mn-1)/2}\tau\bigl((mn-1)/(2e)\bigr)^2
                            \leq\tau(mn-1)^3.$$ Collecting by $v=mn$ and using [lem:divisor-moments](#lem:divisor-moments), we obtain <a id="eq:typeii-diagonal"></a>$$\label{eq:typeii-diagonal}
 \mathcal N_{\rm diag}
 \ll L^{O(1)}\sum_{v\asymp x}\tau(v)\tau(v-1)^3
 \ll xL^{C_5}.$$ The exponents $C_3,C_5$ are independent of $K_0$. Since $EV\asymp xL^{-K_0}$, its contribution to $|B_0|^2$ is $O(x^2L^{-K_0+C_3+C_5})$. We can therefore fix $K_0$ large enough that its square root, even multiplied by $L^{C_{f}}$, is $O(xL^{-D_*-1})$. All subsequent precisions may depend on this fixed $K_0$.

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### The exact determinant parametrization

Consider an off-diagonal term of [eq:typeii-cauchy](#eq:typeii-cauchy), with <a id="eq:typeii-two-equations"></a>$$\label{eq:typeii-two-equations}
       mn=2eh+1,\qquad m'n=2eh'+1,\qquad m\ne m'.$$ The shared variables $e,n$ force the two $m$ values to be congruent modulo $2e$: the first equation makes $n$ a unit modulo $2e$, and subtracting the equations gives $2e\mid m'-m$. Write $m'-m=2ek$, with $k\ne0$. The resulting identities are <a id="eq:typeii-determinant"></a>$$\label{eq:typeii-determinant}
 m'-m=2ek,\qquad h'-h=kn,\qquad
 z=m'h,\qquad z+k=mh'.$$ The last identity follows from $mh'-m'h=k(mn-2eh)=k$. The enlarged $e$ range gives <a id="eq:typeii-k-range"></a>$$\label{eq:typeii-k-range}
                  0<|k|\ll L^{K_0+C_s}.$$

Conversely, fix such a $k$, positive integers $m,m',h,h'$ with $P^{-}(m),P^{-}(m')>W$, and representations $m'h=z$, $mh'=z+k$. Suppose $$m'\equiv m\pmod{2|k|},\qquad
                 e=\frac{m'-m}{2k}>0.$$ Then $e$ is an integer and <a id="eq:typeii-converse-identity"></a>$$\label{eq:typeii-converse-identity}
                   m(h'-h)=k(2eh+1).$$ Every prime factor of $m$ exceeds $W$, whereas $|k|$ is bounded by a fixed power of $L$. Hence $(m,k)=1$ for sufficiently large $x$. [eq:typeii-converse-identity](#eq:typeii-converse-identity) implies $m\mid2eh+1$. Set $n=(2eh+1)/m$, a positive integer. The same identity gives $h'-h=kn$, and substituting $m'=m+2ek$ recovers the second equation of [eq:typeii-two-equations](#eq:typeii-two-equations). This proves a bijection, with all variables recovered by the displayed formulas. In particular, no additional divisibility condition is being dropped. The coprimality $(m,k)=1$ is necessary to this converse.

displays the cross-pairing that produces the two shifted endpoints.

<figure id="fig:determinant">

<figcaption>Forward determinant identities for factorizations with common $e, n$. Cross-paired factors give $z = m′h$ and $z + k = mh′$; the vertical arrows record signed increments, so either sign of $k$ is allowed. The converse reconstruction, including its congruence, positivity, and coprimality conditions, is proved in the text.</figcaption>
</figure>

Both $m$ and $m'$ are units modulo $q_k=2|k|$, so the congruence is imposed exactly by <a id="eq:typeii-character-congruence"></a>$$\label{eq:typeii-character-congruence}
 \mathbf 1_{m'\equiv m\ (q_k)}
       =\frac1{\varphi(q_k)}\sum_{\chi\bmod q_k}
                                  \chi(m)\overline{\chi(m')}.$$ Thus $\mathcal N_{\rm off}$ is a sum over $k,z$ and these characters of terms having coefficient $$\alpha_m\overline{\alpha_{m'}}\chi(m)\overline{\chi(m')}
                    a_h(h)\overline{a_h(h')},
 \qquad m'h=z,\quad mh'=z+k,$$ with the enlarged smooth $(e,n)$ cutoffs. Their arguments are now the exact functions <a id="eq:typeii-pulled-back"></a>$$\label{eq:typeii-pulled-back}
       e=\frac{m'-m}{2k},\qquad
       n=\frac{(m'-m)z}{kmm'}+\frac1m.$$ The cutoffs are extended smoothly by zero to nonpositive arguments, so they impose the positivity and size conditions as well.

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
#### Smooth separation and the shifted endpoints

On the support just obtained, $m,m'\asymp U$, $n\asymp V$, and $h\asymp x/e$. Hence $z=m'h$ lies in $[xL^{-C_6},xL^{C_6}]$ for a fixed $C_6$ now allowed to depend on $K_0$. Insert a smooth dyadic partition in $z$ with $O(T)$ terms, and denote one scale by $Z=xL^{O(1)}$. Keep its smooth cutoff $\psi_Z(z/Z)$ outside the ensuing Fourier expansion.

For completeness, the pulled-back cutoffs have the following uniform regularity. Put $t_1=\log(m/U)$, $t_2=\log(m'/U)$, $t_3=\log(z/Z)$. Multiply by fixed smooth localizations in these three variables equal to one on the ranges in use. From [eq:typeii-pulled-back](#eq:typeii-pulled-back), every derivative of $e/E$ in the $t_i$ is $O_j(U/(|k|E))$, and every derivative of $n/V$ is $$O_j\bigl(Z/(|k|UV)+(UV)^{-1}\bigr).$$ Both are bounded by fixed powers of $L$; moreover, the normalized derivatives of $\eta_e,\eta_n$ have the bounds already given. Repeated use of the chain rule therefore bounds derivatives of the localized product cutoff by $O_j(L^{C_7(j+1)})$. Its support in $(t_1,t_2,t_3)$ is a fixed compact box. Fourier inversion, as in [lem:fourier-separation](#lem:fourier-separation), consequently represents this cutoff with total variation $L^{O(1)}$ as a superposition of <a id="eq:typeii-three-powers"></a>$$\label{eq:typeii-three-powers}
                        m^{i\xi_1}(m')^{i\xi_2}z^{i\xi_3}.$$ Here is an explicit tail estimate. Enlarge a fixed exponent $C_8$ so that, with $P=L^{C_8}$, the localized cutoff $F$ satisfies $\|\partial^\alpha F\|_\infty\ll_\alpha P^{|\alpha|}$; its zeroth derivative is bounded independently of $L$. Integration by parts then gives $$|\widehat F(\xi)|\ll_j(1+|\xi|/P)^{-j},\qquad
 \int_{\mathbb R^3}|\widehat F(\xi)|\,d\xi\ll P^3,\qquad
 \int_{|\xi|>PL}|\widehat F(\xi)|\,d\xi\ll_j P^3L^{3-j}.$$ Thus truncation at $PL$ has uniform error $O(L^{-A})$ for every fixed $A$, by choosing $j>A+3C_8+3$. These later exponents may depend on $K_0$.

There is sufficient absolute control to sum these errors. If the coefficients in [eq:typeii-separated-coefficients](#eq:typeii-separated-coefficients) and the two $m$ sequences are replaced by their absolute values, each resulting endpoint convolution is at most $L^{O(1)}\tau(z_*)^2$. Its product with $W_\ell(z)$ has squared sum $O(ZL^{O(1)})$ on $z\asymp Z$. Cauchy’s inequality, also at $z+k$, therefore bounds the absolute correlation by $ZL^{O(1)}$. This uses only [lem:divisor-moments](#lem:divisor-moments). It remains true after dropping the congruence; hence it controls the errors just described, after summing the polynomial number of $k$ values, dyads, and character terms.

We spell out the endpoint identification to track exactly which sequence satisfies discrepancy. Use the notation of [eq:typeii-separated-coefficients](#eq:typeii-separated-coefficients) and fix a character $\chi$ and frequencies in [eq:typeii-three-powers](#eq:typeii-three-powers). Since $m,m'$ are free of group primes, $$z_*=m'h_*,\qquad (z+k)_*=mh'_*,\qquad
       W_\ell(z)=W_\ell(h),\quad W_\ell(z+k)=W_\ell(h').$$ Define the invariant endpoint functions <a id="eq:typeii-first-endpoint"></a><a id="eq:typeii-second-endpoint"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 F_0(v)&=\sum_{mpr=v_*}
   \alpha_m\chi(m)m^{i(\varrho-\xi_2)}
   \mathbf 1_{p\in I_{**},\,P^{-}(p)>W}p^{-i\sigma}\overline{c_r},
          \label{eq:typeii-first-endpoint}\\
 G_0(v)&=\sum_{mpr=v_*}
   \alpha_m\chi(m)m^{i(\xi_1+\varrho)}
   \mathbf 1_{p\in I_{**},\,P^{-}(p)>W}p^{-i\sigma}\overline{c_r}.
          \label{eq:typeii-second-endpoint}
\end{aligned}$$ Direct substitution shows that the separated summand is $$\psi_Z(z/Z)z^{i(\varrho+\xi_3)}(z+k)^{-i\varrho}
       \overline{F_0(z)}G_0(z+k)W_\ell(z)W_\ell(z+k),$$ up to scalar phases of modulus one and the Fourier coefficient. For example, at $z=m'h$ the factor $h^{i\varrho}$ becomes $z^{i\varrho}(m')^{-i\varrho}$, which explains the first endpoint’s power and the conjugation of $c_r$. In particular, the sequence inside $F_0$ is the original $\alpha$, with its actual interval restriction.

Both [eq:typeii-first-endpoint](#eq:typeii-first-endpoint), [eq:typeii-second-endpoint](#eq:typeii-second-endpoint) have exactly the form in [eq:endpoint-form](#eq:endpoint-form). The character modulus $2|k|$, all power frequencies, and the coefficient bounds are fixed powers of $L$. The interval $I_{**}$ lies in $[x^\tau,x^\eta]$ for fixed $0<\tau<\eta<1/4$, by [eq:typeii-rough-slot](#eq:typeii-rough-slot) and the hypothesis $\eta_*<b_*/4$. The residual $c_r$ has a fixed power bound, and no roughness condition on $r$ is needed. The original $\alpha$ is supported on $W$-rough integers and satisfies [eq:discrepancy](#eq:discrepancy); the explicit character and power factors are precisely the factors permitted in [eq:endpoint-form](#eq:endpoint-form). Thus the discrepancy assumption has not been silently transferred to a new coefficient sequence.

Finally set $\widetilde\psi_Z(t)=t\psi_Z(t)$. The last unweighted correlation equals $Z$ times $$\sum_{z>0,\ z+k>0}\frac{\widetilde\psi_Z(z/Z)}z
 z^{i(\varrho+\xi_3)}(z+k)^{-i\varrho}
       \overline{F_0(z)}G_0(z+k)W_\ell(z)W_\ell(z+k).$$ This is [eq:shift-bound](#eq:shift-bound), including its harmonic normalization. Given any fixed $D_0$, [thm:shift](#thm:shift) bounds it by $L^{-D_0}$ once the discrepancy exponent $B$ is sufficiently large. Consequently each separated unweighted correlation is $O(ZL^{-D_0})$. Choose $D_0$ after $K_0$, the $k$ range, all separation costs, and the desired saving in [eq:typeii-cauchy](#eq:typeii-cauchy). The total off-diagonal contribution is then $O(xL^{-A})$ for any prescribed fixed $A$. Choose $B$ after this application of [thm:shift](#thm:shift). Together with [eq:typeii-diagonal](#eq:typeii-diagonal), this gives $|B_0|\ll xL^{-D_*-C_{f}-1}$. Summing its pre-Cauchy expansion and the previously estimated tails proves [eq:typeii-bound](#eq:typeii-bound). ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-typeii-order"></a>
<div id="rem:typeii-order" class="remark">

**Remark 32** (Order of the precisions). The band constants, $j_{**}$, $b_*$, coefficient bound $C$, and target $D_*$ are fixed first. The transition functions, their total variation, and the coefficient and diagonal exponents are independent of $K_0$; only then is $K_0$ chosen. The smooth separation after Cauchy’s inequality and the accuracy required from [thm:shift](#thm:shift) are fixed next, and the required discrepancy exponent $B$ is fixed last. Accordingly, when $\alpha$ is the difference between a test and its rough-integer proxy, the precision of the cells defining that proxy may be chosen after $B$, provided the proxy’s coefficient bound is already uniform in that cell precision. This is the order used in [sec:primes](#sec:primes).

</div>

<a id="sec:primes"></a>
### Extracting primes with smooth predecessors

We prove [thm:smooth](#thm:smooth). Throughout this section $0<\delta<1/4$ is fixed. The task is to detect primes among $N_u:=2u+1$ for a positive weight on integers $u$ all of whose prime factors are small. The distribution estimate of [thm:typeii](#thm:typeii) will replace tests on a factor of $N_u$ by simpler tests on rough integers. We specify the order of all remaining parameter choices at the end of the section.

The extraction separates the composites according to their least prime factor. A preliminary sieve retains those $N_u$ with no prime factor below $x^{b_1}$, where $b_1>0$ is fixed and small. For a fixed small $\kappa>0$ and least prime factors up to $x^{1/2-\kappa}$, Type II replaces the roughness condition on the complementary factor by a local density on ordinary integers. Integrating these densities accounts for the composite part of the sieve mass. The remaining composites have two prime factors close to $x^{1/2}$; a separate upper-bound sieve makes their contribution small with $\kappa$. Its constant must be independent of $\kappa$, so we will establish that uniformity before making the final choices.

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
#### The candidate and its mass

<a id="eq:candidate-geometry"></a>
Fix a nonnegative smooth function $\Psi$ supported in $[1,2]$ that is bounded below by a positive constant on a closed interval of positive length contained in $(1,2)$. In the marked weight of [sec:shifts](#sec:shifts) take $q=1/2$ and $\ell=1$. Set $$c_1=1,\quad c_2=\frac65,\quad c_3=\frac32,\quad c_4=\frac95,
 \qquad s'=\frac1{r_0(c_1+c_2)},\qquad s_j=s'2^{-j}L,$$ where the fixed integer $r_0$ is sufficiently large that $$\label{eq:candidate-geometry}
 c_2s'<\delta,\qquad \frac{(r_0-1)c_1}{2}\ge c_2+1.$$ Let $j_x$ be the largest integer with $c_1s_{j_x}\ge20T$, and let $$Q_j=\{p\text{ prime}:c_1s_j\le\log p\le c_2s_j\}
 \qquad(0\le j\le j_x).$$ At each scale for which $[c_3s_j,c_4s_j]$ lies wholly in $[L^{1/10},L^{1/5}]$ or $[L^{3/10},L^{2/5}]$, take the primes in that interval of logarithms as a small or big P-group, respectively. The P-groups and the Q-bands are pairwise disjoint: the four constants lie in the displayed order, and $c_4<2c_1$. The prime number theorem and partial summation give fixed upper and positive lower bounds for each of their reciprocal prime masses. Both numbers of P-groups are comparable to $T$, and their exponent ranges and endpoint ratios meet the hypotheses of the preceding sections.

There are $r_0$ ordered slots in each Q-band. Except for one designated slot in a fixed band $j_{**}$, a slot contains a prime of its band. The designated slot instead contains any integer $v$ satisfying $$e^{c_1s_{j_{**}}}\le v\le e^{c_2s_{j_{**}}},\qquad P^{-}(v)>W.$$ We always require $j_{**}\ge j_0$, where $j_0$ is a sufficiently large fixed lower bound chosen below using only the candidate geometry. In particular $j_{**}\ge1$. Define $$a_Q(v)=\prod_{j=0}^{j_x}\frac1{r_0!}
       \#\{\text{admissible ordered Q-lists with product }v\},
 \qquad A(u)=a_Q(u_*)W_1(u).$$ The P-free part $u_*$ and the marked weight $W_1$ are those of [sec:shifts](#sec:shifts). The rough slot has no P-prime divisor for large $x$, since all P primes are less than $W$.

The candidate must have substantial ordinary mass near $x$, despite the restrictions on all its factors. Reciprocal weights make the slot choices independent. The band geometry leaves room for one prime in the largest band to adjust their product to size $x$; under this harmonic sampling, that prime supplies a local probability of order $1/L$. The next proposition records the resulting mass and the pointwise bound needed to convert that mass into a count of distinct primes.

<a id="stmt-prop-candidate-mass"></a>
<div id="prop:candidate-mass" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:candidate-pointwise"></a>
<a id="eq:candidate-mass"></a>
**Proposition 33** (Candidate mass). *Put $$X_A=\sum_u A(u)\Psi(u/x),\quad
 H_Q=\sum_v\frac{a_Q(v)}v,\quad
 H_P=\sum_{r:r_*=1}\frac{W_1(r)}r.$$ There are constants $C,C_A$ depending only on the early candidate choices such that, for sufficiently large $x$ after fixing $j_{**}$, $$\begin{aligned}
 0\le A(u)&\le L^C\tau(u_*),
 & A(u)&\le\exp(C\sqrt L)\quad(u\asymp x),\label{eq:candidate-pointwise}\\
 H_P&\sim\exp\left(q\sum_g V_g\right),
 & X_A&\asymp\frac{x}{L}H_QH_P\gg xL^{-C_A}.
 \label{eq:candidate-mass}
\end{aligned}$$ The comparison constants in <a href="#eq:candidate-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:candidate-mass">[eq:candidate-mass]</a> can be chosen independently of $j_{**}\ge j_0$. On the support of $A(u)\Psi(u/x)$ every prime divisor of $u$ lies in $[L^{20},x^\delta]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-candidate-mass"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-candidate-mass</summary>

*Proof.* After fixing the value of the rough slot as a divisor of $v$, the remaining prime multiplicities determine their band assignments. The normalized number of ordered lists in each band is at most one. This gives $a_Q(v)\le\tau(v)$. For $v\ll x$, any possible rough-slot divisor divides the part of $v$ supported on primes exceeding $W$. That part has at most $O(L/\log W)=O(\sqrt L)$ prime factors counted with multiplicity, and hence at most $\exp(O(\sqrt L))$ divisors. Together with the pointwise bound $W_1(u)\le L^{O(1)}$ this proves <a href="#eq:candidate-pointwise" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:candidate-pointwise">[eq:candidate-pointwise]</a>. The claimed lower and upper bounds on prime factors follow from the definitions, <a href="#eq:candidate-geometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:candidate-geometry">[eq:candidate-geometry]</a>, and $\exp(L^{2/5})<x^{c_2s'}$ for large $x$.

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
###### Harmonic masses and the P-part.

<a id="eq:candidate-harmonic-size"></a>
The Q harmonic sum factorizes into the reciprocal masses of all its slots, divided by $r_0!$ in each band. Each pure-prime slot has mass between two fixed positive constants. The rough-slot mass is at most $O(L)$ and is bounded below by a fixed positive constant for sufficiently large $x$ after fixing $j_{**}$; for the latter assertion one may restrict that slot to primes of $Q_{j_{**}}$, which then all exceed $W$. Since there are $O(T)$ slots, $$\label{eq:candidate-harmonic-size}
 L^{-C}\le H_Q\le L^C.$$ This exponent and these eventual bounds do not depend on $j_{**}$.

<a id="eq:marked-euler-factor"></a>
<a id="eq:mask-euler-comparison"></a>
For one P-group write $F_g(q)=\prod_{p\in\mathcal P_g}(1+q/(p-1))$. Summing over all powers of its prime divisors shows that its factor in $H_P$ is $$\label{eq:marked-euler-factor}
 \frac{F_g'(q)}{V_g}
  =F_g(q)\frac1{V_g}\sum_{p\in\mathcal P_g}\frac1{p-1+q}.$$ As $\sum_{g,p\in\mathcal P_g}p^{-2}=o(1)$ and $V_g$ is bounded above and below, multiplying <a href="#eq:marked-euler-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marked-euler-factor">[eq:marked-euler-factor]</a> proves the asymptotic for $H_P$. It also proves the useful exact inequality $$\label{eq:mask-euler-comparison}
 H_P\ge\prod_{p\text{ a P prime}}\left(1+\frac q{p-1}\right),$$ since $p-1+q\le p$.

<a id="eq:candidate-P-tail"></a>
The harmonic P-measure assigns negligible mass to $r>x^\sigma$ for any fixed $\sigma>0$. Indeed set $\xi=L^{-2/5}$ and replace $p^{-1}$ by $p^{-1+\xi}$ in the preceding Euler calculation. Since $p^\xi\le e$ for every P prime, the tilted factor of each group is bounded by a fixed constant. Consequently $$\label{eq:candidate-P-tail}
 \sum_{r>x^\sigma,\ r_*=1}\frac{W_1(r)}r
 \le \exp(-\sigma L^{3/5})L^{O(1)}.$$

<a id="pm-h-49"></a>
###### Localizing the product near $x$.

Normalize the independent P choice and all the Q-slot harmonic choices by $H_PH_Q$, and denote their product by $U$. Then $$X_A=H_PH_Q\,\mathbb E\{U\Psi(U/x)\}.$$ For the upper comparison we need probability $O(1/L)$ throughout $x\le U\le2x$; for the lower comparison we need probability $\gg1/L$ where $U/x$ lies in the positivity interval of $\Psi$. Both are intervals of bounded length for $\log U$. The P-tail estimate ensures that the P-part does not move the product out of reach of the largest Q-band. Condition on everything except one pure-prime slot in $Q_0$. The prime number theorem implies that its logarithm falls in any interval of bounded length with probability $O(1/L)$, uniformly in the location of that interval. Thus $\mathbb P(x\le U\le2x)=O(1/L)$, giving the upper mass bound.

Here is a uniform lower bound. The sum of the midpoints of all the infinite Q-bands, counting their $r_0$ slots, is exactly $$\sum_{j\ge0}r_0\frac{c_1+c_2}{2}s'2^{-j}L=L.$$ Let $\Delta=(c_2-c_1)s'L$, the logarithmic width of the free slot. Choose a fixed prefix of bands so long that the sum of all later band widths and midpoints is less than $\Delta/24$. Within that prefix, restrict all slots except the free slot to fixed small relative neighborhoods of their midpoints, so that their total deviation is at most $\Delta/24$. This event has probability bounded below by a positive constant: it involves only a fixed number of pure-prime slots. Choose $j_0$ larger than the prefix length so that the exceptional slot is always in the unrestricted tail. Its full range obeys the same deterministic tail bound. Missing bands after $j_x$ also obey that bound. Finally restrict the P choice to $\log r\le\Delta/12$, at negligible loss by <a href="#eq:candidate-P-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:candidate-P-tail">[eq:candidate-P-tail]</a>. On this event, a target interval for $\log U$ coming from the positivity interval of $\Psi$ gives an interval of fixed positive length for the free slot, lying a distance at least $\Delta/4$ from its range endpoints for large $x$. The prime number theorem gives conditional probability $\gg1/L$. Multiplying by $U\asymp x$ proves the lower comparison in <a href="#eq:candidate-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:candidate-mass">[eq:candidate-mass]</a>. This argument uses no property of the tail distribution beyond its range, so its constants are uniform in the later choice of $j_{**}$. The final lower bound follows from <a href="#eq:candidate-harmonic-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:candidate-harmonic-size">[eq:candidate-harmonic-size]</a> and the Euler calculation. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-50"></a>
#### Congruence distribution and the preliminary sieve

For odd squarefree $d$ define $$r(d)=\sum_{d\mid N_u}A(u)\Psi(u/x)-\frac{X_A}{\varphi(d)}.$$

<a id="stmt-prop-typei"></a>
<div id="prop:typeI" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:typeI-bound"></a>
**Proposition 34** (Type I distribution). *For every fixed $\vartheta<1/2$ and $D>0$, $$\label{eq:typeI-bound}
 \sum_{\substack{d\le x^\vartheta\\d\text{ odd and squarefree}}}
 |r(d)|\ll xL^{-D}+X_AL^{-18}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-typei"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-typei</summary>

*Proof.* The congruence $2u+1\equiv0\pmod d$ specifies a unit class for $u$. Use character orthogonality on that class. Replacing the principal coprime mass by $X_A$ has total cost at most $X_AL^{-18}$, because for each supported $u$, $$\sum_{\substack{d\le x^\vartheta\\(d,u)>1}}
       \frac{\mu^2(d)}{\varphi(d)}
 \le \sum_{p\mid u}\frac1{p-1}
             \sum_{d'\le x}\frac{\mu^2(d')}{\varphi(d')}
 \ll L^{-18}.$$ Here every $p\mid u$ is at least $L^{20}$, there are at most $O(L/\log L)$ such primes, and the last sum is $O(L)$ by its Euler product and Mertens’ theorem.

Extract one fixed $Q_0$ slot, as in the proof of [prop:candidate-mass](#prop:candidate-mass), to write $$A(u)=\sum_{\substack{pr=u\\p\in Q_0}}b(r),
 \qquad 0\le b(r)\le L^{O(1)}\tau(r).$$ Split $p,r$ into dyads of sizes $P,U$ with $PU\asymp x$. There is a fixed $a>0$ such that $x^a\ll P,U\ll x^{1-a}$ in every contributing dyad. A nonprincipal character modulo squarefree $d$ is induced by a primitive nonprincipal character modulo $f>1$, where $d=hf$ and $(h,f)=1$. It acts as that primitive character together with the restrictions $(p,h)=(r,h)=1$, and $\varphi(d)=\varphi(h)\varphi(f)$.

Fix $h$. On conductors $f\asymp R>L^{C'}$, separate $\Psi(pr/x)$ by Mellin inversion, which has bounded integrated absolute cost. For each resulting twist, [thm:large-sieve](#thm:large-sieve), Cauchy–Schwarz, and [lem:divisor-moments](#lem:divisor-moments) give $$\begin{aligned}
 &\sum_{f\asymp R}\frac1{\varphi(f)}
     \sum_{\chi\bmod f}^{*}
     \left|\sum_{p\asymp P}a_p\chi(p)\right|
     \left|\sum_{r\asymp U}b_r\chi(r)\right|\\
 &\hspace{12mm}\ll
  \frac{L^{O(1)}}R\sqrt{(P+R^2)(U+R^2)PU}\\
 &\hspace{12mm}\ll xL^{O(1)}
       \left(R^{-1}+P^{-1/2}+U^{-1/2}+R/\sqrt x\right).
\end{aligned}$$ The coefficients include coprimality to $h$ and unit-modulus Mellin twists, so their squared sums are $O(P)$ and $UL^{O(1)}$. Discarding $(h,f)=1$ and squarefreeness only enlarges this positive bound. Summing dyads and $1/\varphi(h)$ costs a fixed power of $L$. Since $R\le x^\vartheta$, a sufficiently large $C'$ gives any prescribed logarithmic saving.

For $1<f\le2L^{C'}$, fix $r$ and apply [thm:sw](#thm:sw) to the $p$-sum with the original smooth cutoff $\Psi(pr/x)$, using partial summation. The nonprincipal character sum is $O_A(PL^{-A})$ for arbitrary fixed $A$. Omitting primes dividing $h$ costs only $O(1)$ terms, since such primes in this range are at least $x^a$ and $h\le x^\vartheta$. Their total cost after summing $r$, moduli and dyads is $x^{1-a}L^{O(1)}$. The remaining costs are fixed powers of $L$, absorbed by choosing $A$ large. This proves <a href="#eq:typeI-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:typeI-bound">[eq:typeI-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:sieve-products"></a>
<a id="eq:rough-slot-placement"></a>
<a id="eq:presieve-mass"></a>
Define $$V(y)=\prod_{p\le y}(1-1/p),\qquad
 V_1(y)=\prod_{3\le p\le y}(1-1/(p-1)),\qquad
 \mathfrak S=2\prod_{p\ge3}\left(1-\frac1{(p-1)^2}\right)>0.$$ Writing $\gamma_E$ for Euler’s constant, Mertens’ theorem gives $$\label{eq:sieve-products}
 V(y)\sim\frac{e^{-\gamma_E}}{\log y},\qquad
 V_1(y)\sim\mathfrak S V(y).$$ We will choose a small fixed $0<\kappa<1/50$ and then a sufficiently small fixed $b_1>0$. Set $b_*=b_1/3$ for [thm:typeii](#thm:typeii), and eventually require $$\label{eq:rough-slot-placement}
 j_{**}\ge j_0,\qquad c_2s'2^{-j_{**}}<b_*/4.$$ Apply [lem:sieve](#lem:sieve) to odd prime divisors of $N_u$ up to $x^{b_1}$, with density $g(p)=1/(p-1)$ and the remainders from [prop:typeI](#prop:typeI). Take $$h=2\left\lfloor\frac1{40b_1}\right\rfloor,
 \qquad \vartheta=\frac12-\frac\kappa2.$$ For sufficiently small $b_1$, $h$ is large enough for that lemma and $b_1(4h+2)<\vartheta$. Choose $D>C_A+10$ in <a href="#eq:typeI-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:typeI-bound">[eq:typeI-bound]</a>. It follows that $$\label{eq:presieve-mass}
 \sum_{P^{-}(N_u)>x^{b_1}}A(u)\Psi(u/x)
 \ge \frac{\mathfrak S X_A}{L}
   \left\{\frac{e^{-\gamma_E}}{b_1}
                 (1-O(e^{-c/b_1}))+o(1)\right\}.$$ The constants in the exponential error are uniform for small $b_1$ and $0<\kappa<1/50$.

<a id="pm-h-51"></a>
#### Proxies with character and Mellin discrepancy

We now construct the coefficients that Type II will compare with the factor tests. The proxy has exactly the same sum as the test on each short logarithmic cell, but is constant among the $W$-rough integers in that cell. This count matching handles the principal characters; prime estimates and the elementary sieve will give the nonprincipal cancellation independently of Type II. The cell width may therefore be chosen after Type II specifies its required discrepancy.

<a id="eq:proxy-definition"></a>
At fixed $b_1,\kappa$, partition $(b_1,1/2-\kappa]$ into finitely many exponent bins $(\gamma,\gamma']$. Their mesh will be chosen later. On the factor ranges used below consider the tests $$I_\gamma(m)=\mathbf 1_{P^{-}(m)>x^\gamma},\qquad
 I_{\mathrm{pr}}(m)=\mathbf 1_{m\text{ prime}}.$$ Every such range lies between two fixed positive powers of $x$; in particular we can work throughout the ambient interval $[x^{b_1/4},x^{1-b_1/4}]$. Put $\Delta_L=L^{-S}$, with $S$ a fixed precision to be chosen last. Partition the logarithmic axis into intervals of width $\Delta_L$. For a full cell $C_Y=(Y,e^{\Delta_L}Y]$ meeting this ambient interval, set $$\label{eq:proxy-definition}
 c_I(C_Y)=\frac{\sum_{m\in C_Y}I(m)}
                   {\#\{m\in C_Y:P^{-}(m)>W\}},\qquad
 B_I(m)=c_I(C_Y)\mathbf 1_{P^{-}(m)>W}\quad(m\in C_Y).$$ The counts defining $c_I$ are full-cell counts even when the factor range cuts a cell. Range restrictions are imposed afterwards.

<a id="stmt-lem-proxy-discrepancy"></a>
<div id="lem:proxy-discrepancy" class="lemma">

**Lemma 35** (Proxy discrepancy). *The denominators in <a href="#eq:proxy-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:proxy-definition">[eq:proxy-definition]</a> are positive for large $x$. Moreover $0\le B_I\le1$. Given any fixed discrepancy exponent $B$, taking $S>2B+3$ makes $$\alpha_m=(I(m)-B_I(m))\mathbf 1_{m\in K}$$ satisfy <a href="#eq:discrepancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:discrepancy">[eq:discrepancy]</a>, uniformly for every interval $K$ inside a dyad in the stated factor ranges, for each of the finitely many tests above. Thus the coefficient bound required by [thm:typeii](#thm:typeii) is independent of $S$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-proxy-discrepancy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-proxy-discrepancy</summary>

<a id="eq:proxy-denominator"></a>
*Proof.* Use [lem:sieve](#lem:sieve) on ordinary integers in a cell with $z=W$, $g(p)=1/p$, and $h=2\lceil T^2\rceil$. Each divisibility remainder is $O(1)$, and the sieve level is $$D_W=W^{4h+2}=\exp(O(\sqrt L\,T^2))=x^{o(1)}.$$ Since $Y\gg x^{b_1/4}$, uniformly for these cells, $$\label{eq:proxy-denominator}
 \#\{m\in C_Y:P^{-}(m)>W\}
  =(e^{\Delta_L}-1)YV(W)(1+O(e^{-T^2}))+O(D_W)
  \sim\Delta_L YV(W).$$ Both actual tests select subsets of these rough integers for large $x$: for the prime test all integers in the cell exceed $W$, and for the other tests $x^\gamma>W$. Hence $0\le c_I\le1$ exactly.

Every prime divisor of a modulus $r\le L^B$ is below $W$, so a principal character is one throughout the support of $\alpha$. On a full cell the unweighted sum of $I-B_I$ is zero. For $f(t)=t^{iv-1}$, $|v|\le L^B$, its variation on that cell is at most $O((1+|v|)\Delta_L/Y)$. The absolute harmonic mass on a dyad is $O(1)$ (even the weaker $O(L^{1/2})$ would suffice). Consequently the full cells contribute $O(L^{B-S})$. Intersecting the test interval in <a href="#eq:discrepancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:discrepancy">[eq:discrepancy]</a> with $K$ leaves at most two partial cells; their combined harmonic mass is $O(L^{-S}+x^{-b_1/4})$. These bounds imply the principal-character case with room to spare when $S>2B+3$.

For a nonprincipal character, first treat the actual roughness test. Each admitted integer has a unique nondecreasing list of at most $O(1/b_1)$ prime factors. Fix the first $l-1$ factors and sum over the last prime. Its order restriction, its lower bound $x^\gamma$, and the interval restriction on the full product intersect in one prime interval. Its endpoints lie between $x^\gamma$ and $x^2$. By [thm:sw](#thm:sw) and partial summation, the harmonic sum of this prime against a nonprincipal character and the twist $p^{iv}$ has arbitrarily strong logarithmic saving, uniformly for a prescribed logarithmic bound on the modulus and $v$. The reciprocal masses of the remaining factors are bounded in terms of $b_1$, because $\sum_{x^\gamma<p\le x^2}p^{-1}=O_{b_1}(1)$. Summing the bounded number of list lengths preserves the saving. Repeated primes are included by the nondecreasing enumeration. Imprimitive nonprincipal characters give the same estimate via their nonprincipal primitive characters; primes dividing the modulus are too small to occur. For $I_{\mathrm{pr}}$ this is the same argument with one prime.

It remains to treat the proxy. For every subinterval $J'$ of a cell and every unit class $a\pmod r$, apply [lem:sieve](#lem:sieve) to that class, omitting primes dividing $r$ from the sieve. The CRT gives an $O(1)$ remainder at each squarefree sieve product. Since all prime divisors of $r$ are below $W$, the main term is $$\frac{|J'|}{r}\prod_{\substack{p\le W\\p\nmid r}}(1-1/p)
 =\frac{|J'|V(W)}{\varphi(r)}.$$ Thus, also for very short $J'$ as an absolute-error assertion, <a id="eq:rough-class-count"></a>$$\label{eq:rough-class-count}
 \#\{m\in J':m\equiv a\pmod r,\ P^{-}(m)>W\}
 =\frac{|J'|V(W)}{\varphi(r)}(1+O(e^{-T^2}))+O(D_W).$$ Summing against a nonprincipal character cancels the common main term, and bounds the remaining sum by $O(|J'|V(W)e^{-T^2}+\varphi(r)D_W)$. Multiply by the cell constant $c_I\le1$ and use partial summation against $m^{iv-1}$. The number of cells is at most $O(L^{S+1})$; the moduli and twists cost fixed powers of $L$. The first error therefore remains smaller than every fixed negative power of $L$, and the second is $x^{-b_1/4+o(1)}$ times a fixed power of $L$. This proves the nonprincipal assertion, including all interval truncations. ◻

</details>

For all later applications, prescribe the saving in [thm:typeii](#thm:typeii) large enough that its errors, after the required dyadic decompositions, are $o(X_A/L)$. Its coefficient exponent is fixed before $S$, by [lem:proxy-discrepancy](#lem:proxy-discrepancy); obtain its required discrepancy exponent and then choose $S$. There are only finitely many test types, so the same $S$ works for all.

<a id="pm-h-52"></a>
#### The local densities and their integral identity

The proxy discrepancy allows replacement of a factor test, while its local density controls the size of the replacement. We need ordinary rough-integer counts for the cofactor left after a least prime factor has been selected. For a product of $l$ primes, their logarithms, divided by $L$, sum to the logarithmic size of that cofactor. This leads to the following finite sum of integrals over those logarithms.

<a id="eq:rough-density-definition"></a>
For $w>\gamma>0$ define $$\label{eq:rough-density-definition}
 D_\gamma(w)=\frac1w+
 \sum_{l\ge2}\frac1{l!}
  \int_{\substack{t_i\ge\gamma\ (1\le i<l)\\
                    \sum_{i<l}t_i\le w-\gamma}}
  \frac1{w-\sum_{i<l}t_i}\prod_{i<l}\frac{dt_i}{t_i}.$$ Only finitely many terms are nonzero. On compact subsets of $w>\gamma>0$ the functions are jointly continuous, including at thresholds $w=l\gamma$: the integrands have bounded denominators and the moving boundaries have measure zero.

These are Buchstab’s rough-number densities in logarithmic coordinates, and the minimum-coordinate decomposition below is the corresponding Buchstab identity (Buchstab 1937) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Buchstab1937](#ref-Buchstab1937)]. We derive the short-cell bounds and integral identity needed here directly.

<a id="stmt-lem-proxy-densities"></a>
<div id="lem:proxy-densities" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:prime-proxy-size"></a>
<a id="eq:rough-proxy-size"></a>
<a id="eq:density-integral"></a>
<a id="eq:density-sieve-upper"></a>
**Lemma 36** (Proxy density bounds). *For the prime proxy in the interval $x^{1/2-2\kappa}\le m\le x^{1/2+2\kappa}$, $$\label{eq:prime-proxy-size}
 c_{\mathrm{pr}}(m)\le\frac{C}{LV(W)},$$ where $C$ is absolute for $0<\kappa<1/50$. If $mn=N_u$ on the support of $\Psi(u/x)$ and $\log n/L\in(\gamma,\gamma']$, then $$\label{eq:rough-proxy-size}
 c_\gamma(m)\le
 \frac{\sup_{\gamma\le a\le\gamma'}D_\gamma(1-a)+o(1)}{LV(W)}.$$ At fixed $b_1$ one also has $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{b_1}^{1/2}D_\alpha(1-\alpha)\frac{d\alpha}{\alpha}
       &=D_{b_1}(1)-1,\label{eq:density-integral}\\
 D_{b_1}(1)&\le\frac{e^{-\gamma_E}}{b_1}
                 (1+O(e^{-c/b_1})).\label{eq:density-sieve-upper}
\end{aligned}$$ The integrand at the endpoint $\alpha=1/2$ is understood by its left limit; changing that endpoint value has no effect.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-proxy-densities"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-proxy-densities</summary>

*Proof.* The prime number theorem with an arbitrarily large logarithmic error, applied at both endpoints of a cell, gives $$\#\{p\in(Y,e^{\Delta_L}Y]\}
   =(1+o(1))\frac{\Delta_LY}{\log Y}.$$ For $w=\log Y/L\in[0.46+o(1),0.54+o(1)]$, divide by <a href="#eq:proxy-denominator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:proxy-denominator">[eq:proxy-denominator]</a> to obtain <a href="#eq:prime-proxy-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-proxy-size">[eq:prime-proxy-size]</a> with an absolute eventual constant. Increasing fixed $S$ changes the threshold for $x$, not that constant.

<a id="eq:rough-cell-density"></a>
More generally, uniformly on compact sets with $\gamma\ge b_1$, $w-\gamma$ bounded below positively, and $w$ bounded above, the number of integers rough above $x^\gamma$ in the cell is at most $$\label{eq:rough-cell-density}
 (D_\gamma(w)+o(1))\frac{\Delta_LY}{L}.$$ To see this, repeated prime factors contribute at most $$\sum_{p>x^\gamma}\left\lfloor\frac{2Y}{p^2}\right\rfloor
 \ll Yx^{-b_1}=o(\Delta_LY/L).$$ Count the squarefree remaining integers by ordered lists of $l$ primes with weight $1/l!$, where $l$ is bounded in terms of $b_1$. Fix the first $l-1$ primes, write $Q=\prod_{i<l}p_i$ and $t_i=\log p_i/L$. A last prime can occur only when $\sum_{i<l}t_i\le w-\gamma+O(\Delta_L/L)$. Dropping its lower cutoff, its count is at most $$(1+o(1))\frac{\Delta_LY}{LQ(w-\sum_{i<l}t_i)}.$$ The prime number theorem is uniform here because $Y/Q\ge x^\gamma/2$, and its logarithmic accuracy is chosen larger than $S+3$. The reciprocal-prime measures for the remaining slots converge to $dt_i/t_i$. A finite grid approximation proves uniform convergence of the resulting integrals: denominators stay bounded away from zero, while strips around the moving hyperplane boundary have uniformly vanishing volume. This is exactly <a href="#eq:rough-density-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rough-density-definition">[eq:rough-density-definition]</a>, proving <a href="#eq:rough-cell-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rough-cell-density">[eq:rough-cell-density]</a>. In the application $w=1-\log n/L+o(1)$ and $w-\gamma\ge2\kappa+o(1)$. Continuity, followed by division by <a href="#eq:proxy-denominator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:proxy-denominator">[eq:proxy-denominator]</a>, proves <a href="#eq:rough-proxy-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rough-proxy-size">[eq:rough-proxy-size]</a>.

For <a href="#eq:density-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:density-integral">[eq:density-integral]</a>, write the $l$th term of $D_{b_1}(1)$, $l\ge2$, on the simplex $$t_1+\cdots+t_l=1,\qquad t_i\ge b_1,
 \qquad \frac1{l!}\frac{dt_1\cdots dt_{l-1}}{t_1\cdots t_l}.$$ This measure is invariant under every permutation of the $l$ coordinates: exchanging a dependent and an independent coordinate has absolute Jacobian one. Except on a null set exactly one coordinate is the minimum, say $\alpha$. Select that coordinate in $l$ ways. Its measure is $d\alpha/\alpha$, and the remaining coordinates, with sum $1-\alpha$ and lower bound $\alpha$, give the $(l-1)$-factor term of $D_\alpha(1-\alpha)$. The coefficient $l/l!$ is precisely $1/(l-1)!$. Summing proves the identity; the one-prime term of $D_{b_1}(1)$ equals one.

For the inequality, first obtain a lower density for ordinary rough integers in $(x,2x]$. Ordered prime lists with weight $1/l!$ assign at most unit mass to every integer, including those with repetitions. For $l\ge2$ restrict the first $l-1$ logarithms to $\sum t_i<1-b_1-\eta$, with $\eta>0$ fixed, and sum the last prime over $(x/Q,2x/Q]$. It is then wholly above the threshold. The prime number theorem and the same reciprocal-measure convergence give the corresponding integrals times $x/L$. Include the one-prime term and let $\eta$ decrease to zero. Null boundaries give $$\liminf_{x\to\infty}\frac Lx
        \#\{x<m\le2x:P^{-}(m)>x^{b_1}\}\ge D_{b_1}(1).$$ On the other hand [lem:sieve](#lem:sieve), with $g(p)=1/p$, $O(1)$ remainders and $h=2\lfloor1/(40b_1)\rfloor$, bounds this count by $$xV(x^{b_1})(1+O(e^{-c/b_1}))+O(x^{b_1(4h+2)}).$$ Its remainder is $o(x/L)$ for small $b_1$, and <a href="#eq:sieve-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sieve-products">[eq:sieve-products]</a> proves <a href="#eq:density-sieve-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:density-sieve-upper">[eq:density-sieve-upper]</a>. ◻

</details>

The term $1$ in <a href="#eq:density-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:density-integral">[eq:density-integral]</a> is the one-prime contribution to $D_{b_1}(1)$. Selecting a least prime factor accounts for all the other terms. Thus the integral provides the composite density to subtract from the preliminary sieve mass, with the one-prime term left over. These are densities for ordinary rough integers used in the proxies; their application to $N_u$ uses Type II followed by Type I and the sieve.

<a id="pm-h-53"></a>
#### Subtracting composites away from balance

Consider a composite $N_u$ counted in <a href="#eq:presieve-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:presieve-mass">[eq:presieve-mass]</a>, whose least prime factor is at most $x^{1/2-\kappa}$. Its least prime factor $p$ lies in one bin $(x^\gamma,x^{\gamma'}]$, and $m=N_u/p$ satisfies $P^{-}(m)>x^\gamma$. Its contribution is therefore bounded by $$\sum_{\substack{mp=N_u\\x^\gamma<p\le x^{\gamma'}\text{ prime}}}
       A(u)\Psi(u/x)I_\gamma(m).$$ All contributing dyads of $m,p$ lie between $x^{b_*}$ and $x^{1-b_*}$ for large $x$, with $b_*=b_1/3$: the factors bounded by constants in $N_u\asymp x$ are absorbed by the strict exponent slack. Apply [thm:typeii](#thm:typeii) with $\alpha=I_\gamma-B_\gamma$ restricted to each actual cofactor range, and $\beta$ the prime indicator restricted to its bin and dyad. [lem:proxy-discrepancy](#lem:proxy-discrepancy) supplies precisely its discrepancy hypothesis. Replacing $I_\gamma$ by $B_\gamma$ costs $o(X_A/L)$ in total.

<a id="eq:unbalanced-bin"></a>
<a id="eq:prime-balanced-mass"></a>
For a prime $p>W$, the condition that $m=N_u/p$ be rough above $W$ is equivalent to $P^{-}(N_u)>W$. By <a href="#eq:rough-proxy-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rough-proxy-size">[eq:rough-proxy-size]</a>, the resulting upper bound reduces to a constant divided by $LV(W)$ times $$\sum_{x^\gamma<p\le x^{\gamma'}}
       \sum_{\substack{p\mid N_u\\P^{-}(N_u)>W}}
                     A(u)\Psi(u/x).$$ For each such $p$, sieve the odd primes up to $W$, using the base mass $X_A/(p-1)$, density $1/(l-1)$, and remainders $r(pd)$. Take $h=2\lceil T^2\rceil$ in [lem:sieve](#lem:sieve). All products $pd$ are at most $x^{1/2-\kappa+o(1)}$, which is below the Type I level $x^{1/2-\kappa/2}$. Moreover different pairs $(p,d)$ give different products: $p>W$ is their unique prime factor above $W$. Thus <a href="#eq:typeI-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:typeI-bound">[eq:typeI-bound]</a> bounds the summed remainders without any divisor multiplicity loss. We obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 &\sum_{x^\gamma<p\le x^{\gamma'}}
       \sum_{\substack{p\mid N_u\\P^{-}(N_u)>W}}
                  A(u)\Psi(u/x)\\
 &\hspace{12mm}=X_AV_1(W)
          \sum_{x^\gamma<p\le x^{\gamma'}}\frac1{p-1}
                   +o(X_AV(W)).
\end{aligned}$$ Since the reciprocal sum tends to $\log(\gamma'/\gamma)$, the composite contribution of the bin is at most $$\label{eq:unbalanced-bin}
 \frac{\mathfrak S X_A}{L}
 \left\{\sup_{\gamma\le a\le\gamma'}D_\gamma(1-a)
                    \log(\gamma'/\gamma)+o(1)\right\}.$$ At fixed $b_1,\kappa$, joint continuity in [lem:proxy-densities](#lem:proxy-densities) permits a sufficiently fine fixed mesh such that the sum of these constants is at most $$\int_{b_1}^{1/2-\kappa}D_\alpha(1-\alpha)
                       \frac{d\alpha}{\alpha}+\frac1{10}.$$ Subtracting <a href="#eq:unbalanced-bin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unbalanced-bin">[eq:unbalanced-bin]</a> from <a href="#eq:presieve-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:presieve-mass">[eq:presieve-mass]</a> and using [eq:density-integral](#eq:density-integral), [eq:density-sieve-upper](#eq:density-sieve-upper) leaves at least $$\label{eq:prime-balanced-mass}
 \frac{\mathfrak S X_A}{L}
      \left\{1-\frac1{10}
               -O(b_1^{-1}e^{-c/b_1})+o(1)\right\}
 \ge \frac{\mathfrak S X_A}{2L}$$ on primes and composites with least prime factor exceeding $x^{1/2-\kappa}$, provided $b_1$ is sufficiently small.

<a id="pm-h-54"></a>
#### A uniform upper bound for balanced composites

<a id="eq:balanced-proxy-bound"></a>
If a composite $N_u\ll x$ has least prime factor above $x^{1/2-\kappa}$, it has exactly two prime factors counted with multiplicity, because $3(1/2-\kappa)>1$. Both factors belong, for large $x$, to $$\mathcal B_\kappa=[x^{1/2-2\kappa},x^{1/2+2\kappa}].$$ We may overcount them by ordered pairs $m,n$ of primes in that range with $mn=N_u$. Replace the first prime indicator by its proxy using [thm:typeii](#thm:typeii), then replace the second using the same theorem with the factor roles exchanged. The remaining coefficient in the second application is a proxy bounded by one. All required discrepancy and range hypotheses follow from [lem:proxy-discrepancy](#lem:proxy-discrepancy), as before. By <a href="#eq:prime-proxy-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-proxy-size">[eq:prime-proxy-size]</a>, the resulting upper bound is $o(X_A/L)$ plus $$\label{eq:balanced-proxy-bound}
 \frac{C}{L^2V(W)^2}
 \sum_{\substack{m,n\in\mathcal B_\kappa,\ mn=2u+1\\
                   P^{-}(m)>W,\ P^{-}(n)>W}}
                     A(u)\Psi(u/x).$$ We now bound this positive sum with a constant independent of $\kappa,b_1,j_{**}$ and $S$.

More precisely, we will show that each dyadic pair with $mn\asymp x$ contributes $O(X_AV(W)^2)$. There are only $O(\kappa L+1)$ such pairs in the indicated range, so the prefactor in <a href="#eq:balanced-proxy-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:balanced-proxy-bound">[eq:balanced-proxy-bound]</a> will give an $O((\kappa+L^{-1})X_A/L)$ bound. To prove the dyadic estimate, we retain a small divisor of $u$ from its Q-slots and P-marks. After discarding the remaining large Q-prime restrictions, a two-variable sieve enforces roughness of $m,n$ and the remaining small-prime restrictions on $u=(mn-1)/2$.

<a id="eq:micro-choice"></a>
Choose a fixed large index $j'$ and then $\theta$ in the gap $$\label{eq:micro-choice}
 c_2s'2^{-j'}<\theta<c_1s'2^{-j'+1},\qquad
 10\theta\le\frac1{20},\qquad
 \sum_{j\ge j'}r_0c_2s'2^{-j}\le\frac1{25}.$$ Such a gap exists because $c_2<2c_1$. These choices depend only on the candidate geometry. Increase the early lower bound $j_0$ to ensure $j_0\ge j'$, so the rough slot is in a band $j\ge j'$. Call these the micro bands. Write $H_Q^{\mathrm{mic}}$ for the Q harmonic mass using just these bands. Omitting the finitely many fixed bands $j<j'$ gives <a id="eq:micro-harmonic-comparison"></a>$$\label{eq:micro-harmonic-comparison}
 H_Q^{\mathrm{mic}}\ll H_Q$$ with a constant independent of $j_{**}$.

<a id="eq:assignment-size"></a>
An assignment consists of the complete ordered Q-lists in the micro bands, including the rough slot, and one marked prime from every P-group. Give it weight $$\lambda_{\mathrm{assgn}}
  =\prod_{j=j'}^{j_x}\frac1{r_0!}\prod_g\frac1{V_g},$$ and let $D_1$ be the product of all its entries, with multiplicity. The Q contribution has size at most $x^{1/25}$ by <a href="#eq:micro-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:micro-choice">[eq:micro-choice]</a>; the P marks have product $\exp(O(TL^{2/5}))=x^{o(1)}$. Thus $$\label{eq:assignment-size}
 D_1\le x^{1/20},\qquad
 \sum_{\mathrm{assgn}}\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{assgn}}}{D_1}
            =H_Q^{\mathrm{mic}},\qquad
 \sum_{\mathrm{assgn}}\lambda_{\mathrm{assgn}}
            \le x^{1/20}H_Q^{\mathrm{mic}}.$$ In the middle identity the harmonic sum of the normalized P mark in each group is exactly $V_g^{-1}\sum p^{-1}=1$.

The damping in $W_1$ can also be retained in this upper bound. Once one P-prime per group is marked in $D_1$, every additional distinct P-prime divisor of $u$ contributes a factor $q$. We represent these factors by independent random permissions for those primes; averaging the permissions recovers exactly the damping.

<a id="eq:mask-majorant"></a>
For each assignment, ban every odd non-P prime $l\le x^\theta$ not dividing $D_1$. For each P prime not dividing $D_1$, independently allow it with probability $q$ and otherwise ban it. Primes dividing $D_1$ are never banned. All P primes are below $x^\theta$ and indeed below $W$ for large $x$. We have the pointwise majorant $$\label{eq:mask-majorant}
 A(u)\le
 \sum_{\mathrm{assgn}}\lambda_{\mathrm{assgn}}
     \mathbf 1_{D_1\mid u}\,
     \mathbb E_{\mathrm{mask}}\mathbf 1_{\{l\nmid u\text{ for every banned }l\}}.$$ To verify it, expand $A(u)$ into Q-lists and one P mark per group. For every genuine list the divisibility $D_1\mid u$ holds, and every non-P prime divisor of $u$ below $x^\theta$ occurs in $D_1$: omitted Q-bands contain only pure primes above $x^\theta$. The micro Q product has no P factors, so $D_1$ contains exactly one distinct P prime per group. For such a genuine list, the mask expectation is $q^{\omega(u)-s}$, precisely the damping in $W_1(u)$. Once the micro entries are fixed, the normalized number of omitted pure-prime lists with any fixed residual product is at most one. Explicitly, a band with prime multiplicities $a_p$ contributes $\prod_p1/a_p!\le1$; disjointness prevents alternative band assignments. Dropping these residual restrictions proves <a href="#eq:mask-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mask-majorant">[eq:mask-majorant]</a>.

Fix now a pair of dyads $m\asymp M_1$, $n\asymp M_2$ meeting the product support $mn\asymp x$, an assignment, and a mask. Since $D_1$ is odd, $D_1\mid u$ and $mn=2u+1$ imply $mn\equiv1\pmod{D_1}$. We may drop parity and use this congruence to bound the number of pairs in the full dyadic box. For an odd prime $l\le z':=x^\theta$ with $l\nmid D_1$, the bad events are $$mn\equiv0\pmod l\quad(l\le W),\qquad
 mn\equiv1\pmod l\quad(l\text{ banned}).$$ The two residue sets are disjoint and contain respectively $2l-1$ and $l-1$ pairs. Put $$\nu(l)=(2l-1)\mathbf 1_{l\le W}+(l-1)\mathbf 1_{l\text{ banned}},
 \qquad g(l)=\nu(l)/l^2.$$ These densities satisfy the hypotheses of [lem:sieve](#lem:sieve) uniformly in assignment and mask: $g(l)\le3/l$, and for $l\ge3$ they are bounded away from one. The base mass is the product of the two real side lengths times $\varphi(D_1)/D_1^2$.

<a id="eq:pair-lattice-error"></a>
For a squarefree product $d$ of the sieve primes, the CRT gives exactly $\varphi(D_1)\nu(d)$ residue pairs modulo $D_1d$, including when $D_1$ has prime-power factors. Each pair of residue classes has lattice count equal to its area term with error $$O\left(1+\frac{M_1+M_2}{D_1d}\right).$$ As $\nu(d)\le d\,3^{\omega(d)}$ and $\varphi(D_1)\le D_1$, the sum of absolute remainders up to $D=(z')^{10}=x^{10\theta}$ is $$\label{eq:pair-lattice-error}
 \ll L^{O(1)}\bigl((M_1+M_2)D+D_1D^2\bigr)
 \le L^{O(1)}\bigl((M_1+M_2)x^{1/20}+D_1x^{1/10}\bigr).$$ Here the ordinary $\omega(d)$ occurs only in this elementary divisor bound; its sums follow, for example, from $3^{\omega(d)}\le\tau_3(d)$, where $\tau_3(d)$ counts ordered triples of positive integers with product $d$. Apply the upper-bound version of [lem:sieve](#lem:sieve) with $h=2$.

We record its Euler product explicitly. A banned prime below $W$ has factor $1-3/l+2/l^2$, and a banned prime above $W$ has factor $1-1/l+1/l^2$. Each is at most $$(1-1/l)^{1+2\mathbf 1_{l\le W}}(1+O(l^{-2})).$$ An allowed P prime has factor $(1-1/l)^2$, so it requires one compensating factor $(1-1/l)^{-1}$. Omitting the prime 2 changes only an absolute constant, as does the convergent product of the $1+O(l^{-2})$ factors. Removed primes dividing $D_1$ require at most three compensating factors. Therefore <a id="eq:pair-sieve-product"></a>$$\label{eq:pair-sieve-product}
 \prod_{\substack{3\le l\le z'\\l\nmid D_1}}(1-g(l))
 \le C V(W)^2V(z')
       \prod_{l\mid D_1}(1-1/l)^{-3}
       \prod_{\substack{p\text{ allowed}\\p\nmid D_1}}(1-1/p)^{-1}.$$ Every prime dividing $D_1$ is at least $L^{20}$, so $D_1\le x^{1/20}$ makes its compensating product uniformly bounded. The expectation of the last product is $$\prod_{\substack{p\text{ a P prime}\\p\nmid D_1}}
       \left(1-q+\frac q{1-1/p}\right)
 =\prod_{\substack{p\text{ a P prime}\\p\nmid D_1}}
       \left(1+\frac q{p-1}\right)\le H_P$$ by <a href="#eq:mask-euler-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mask-euler-comparison">[eq:mask-euler-comparison]</a>.

<a id="eq:balanced-dyadic"></a>
Since $\varphi(D_1)/D_1^2\le1/D_1$, sum the main sieve terms over assignments using <a href="#eq:assignment-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:assignment-size">[eq:assignment-size]</a>, and bound $\Psi$ by its fixed supremum. Their contribution on this dyadic pair is $$\ll xV(W)^2V(x^\theta)H_Q^{\mathrm{mic}}H_P
 \ll X_AV(W)^2,$$ by [eq:candidate-mass](#eq:candidate-mass), [eq:micro-harmonic-comparison](#eq:micro-harmonic-comparison) and $V(x^\theta)\ll1/L$. To sum the error <a href="#eq:pair-lattice-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-lattice-error">[eq:pair-lattice-error]</a>, use $M_i\ll x^{0.54}$ and <a href="#eq:assignment-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:assignment-size">[eq:assignment-size]</a>. The result is $$\ll x^{0.64}L^{O(1)}H_Q^{\mathrm{mic}}
       =o(X_AV(W)^2).$$ The comparison is uniform in the later parameters: use $H_Q^{\mathrm{mic}}\ll H_Q$, $H_P\ge1$, and $X_AV(W)^2\asymp xH_QH_P/L^2$. We have proved the promised dyadic estimate $$\label{eq:balanced-dyadic}
 \sum_{\substack{m\asymp M_1,\ n\asymp M_2,\ mn=2u+1\\
                   P^{-}(m)>W,\ P^{-}(n)>W}}
             A(u)\Psi(u/x)\ll X_AV(W)^2.$$

<a id="eq:balanced-final"></a>
There are $O(\kappa L+1)$ possible dyads for $m$ in $\mathcal B_\kappa$, and, for each, only $O(1)$ possible dyads for $n$ because $mn\asymp x$. Combining <a href="#eq:balanced-proxy-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:balanced-proxy-bound">[eq:balanced-proxy-bound]</a> and <a href="#eq:balanced-dyadic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:balanced-dyadic">[eq:balanced-dyadic]</a> bounds the entire balanced composite contribution by $$\label{eq:balanced-final}
 C_{\mathrm{bal}}(\kappa+L^{-1})\frac{X_A}{L}+o(X_A/L).$$ The constant $C_{\mathrm{bal}}$ depends only on $\delta,r_0,\Psi,j',\theta$ and the fixed P-group geometry. It does not depend on $\kappa,b_1,j_{**}$, the later analytic parameters, or $S$. Indeed the candidate comparison is uniform in $j_{**}$; the omitted macro bands are fixed before the gap; the prime proxy bound is uniform on $[0.46,0.54]$; and the sieve-density and lattice constants just used are absolute. Thresholds for $x$ may depend on all parameters after they are fixed.

<a id="pm-h-55"></a>
#### Closing the parameter choices and counting primes

For clarity, the choices in the preceding argument can be made in the following order.

1.  Fix $\delta$, $\Psi$, $r_0$ and the Q/P geometry. Choose the finite prefix for the candidate lower bound, then $j'$ and $\theta$ in <a href="#eq:micro-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:micro-choice">[eq:micro-choice]</a>, and enlarge $j_0$ to cover both requirements. All these are early choices.

2.  Fix $\kappa\in(0,1/50)$ so small that $C_{\mathrm{bal}}\kappa<\mathfrak S/4$. This is possible because the constant in <a href="#eq:balanced-final" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:balanced-final">[eq:balanced-final]</a> is independent of the gap.

3.  Choose $b_1$ sufficiently small for <a href="#eq:presieve-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:presieve-mass">[eq:presieve-mass]</a>, <a href="#eq:density-sieve-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:density-sieve-upper">[eq:density-sieve-upper]</a>, and <a href="#eq:prime-balanced-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-balanced-mass">[eq:prime-balanced-mass]</a>. Set $b_*=b_1/3$. Choose $j_{**}$ to meet <a href="#eq:rough-slot-placement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rough-slot-placement">[eq:rough-slot-placement]</a>, and then choose the finite exponent mesh for <a href="#eq:unbalanced-bin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unbalanced-bin">[eq:unbalanced-bin]</a>.

4.  Prescribe the saving in [thm:typeii](#thm:typeii) so that every replacement error, including dyadic sums, is $o(X_A/L)$. The coefficient exponents are already bounded independently of $S$. The proof of that theorem fixes its Cauchy splitting precision, shift-saving target, graph and analytic parameters, and finally a required discrepancy exponent in <a href="#eq:discrepancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:discrepancy">[eq:discrepancy]</a>.

5.  Choose $S$ sufficiently large in [lem:proxy-discrepancy](#lem:proxy-discrepancy) for that exponent, fix any prime-number-theorem accuracies needed for this $S$, and let $x$ tend to infinity.

In particular the precision of the proxy cells causes no feedback into the Type II coefficient bound.

Subtract <a href="#eq:balanced-final" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:balanced-final">[eq:balanced-final]</a> from <a href="#eq:prime-balanced-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-balanced-mass">[eq:prime-balanced-mass]</a>. For all sufficiently large $x$ there remains a positive constant multiple of $X_A/L$ on prime values of $N_u$: $$\sum_{2u+1\text{ prime}}A(u)\Psi(u/x)\gg X_A/L.$$ By [prop:candidate-mass](#prop:candidate-mass) each summand is at most $\exp(O(\sqrt L))$ and $X_A\gg xL^{-C_A}$. The map $u\mapsto2u+1$ is injective, so the number of distinct primes is at least $$xL^{-C_A-1}\exp(-O(\sqrt L))=x^{1-o(1)}.$$ Their range is $2x<p\le4x+1\le5x$. Finally $P^{+}(p-1)=P^{+}(2u)=\max(2,P^{+}(u))\le x^\delta$ for large $x$; the strict inequality $c_2s'<\delta$ in the candidate geometry leaves room for every subpower P prime, and the factor 2 is eventually below $x^\delta$. This proves [thm:smooth](#thm:smooth).

<a id="sec:totients"></a>
### Large fibers of the totient function

We finish by deriving [thm:totient](#thm:totient) from [thm:smooth](#thm:smooth). This product-and-pigeonhole passage is the classical connection between smooth shifted primes and large totient fibers; see (Pomerance 1980, Theorem B and its proof) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Pomerance1980](#ref-Pomerance1980)]. We first record finiteness of each fiber and the elementary upper bound mentioned in the Introduction.

<a id="stmt-lem-finite-totient-fibers"></a>
<div id="lem:finite-totient-fibers" class="lemma">

**Lemma 37**. *For each positive integer $n$, the set of positive integers $m$ with $\varphi(m)=n$ is finite. For every $\eta>0$, $$g(n)\ll_\eta n^{1+\eta}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-totient-fibers"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-totient-fibers</summary>

*Proof.* If $p^a\parallel m$, then $p^{a-1}(p-1)$ divides $\varphi(m)$. For a fixed value $n$, this restricts $p$ to the finite set with $p-1\mid n$, and bounds $a$ by $p^{a-1}\mid n$. Thus each fiber is finite.

For the quantitative bound, fix $0<\theta<1$. For all sufficiently large primes $p$, one has $1-1/p\ge p^{-\theta}$. The finitely many remaining primes contribute a fixed positive constant $c_\theta$. Consequently, for every $m$, $$\varphi(m)=m\prod_{p\mid m}\left(1-\frac1p\right)
 \ge c_\theta m\prod_{p\mid m}p^{-\theta}
 \ge c_\theta m^{1-\theta}.$$ Every preimage of $n$ is therefore at most $(n/c_\theta)^{1/(1-\theta)}$. Choose $\theta$ so that $1/(1-\theta)\le1+\eta$, and count the possible positive integers. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-totient-fibers-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-totient-fibers</summary>

*Proof of [thm:totient](#thm:totient).* It suffices to consider $0<\varepsilon<1$. Choose $0<\delta<1/4$ with $4\delta<\varepsilon$. By [thm:smooth](#thm:smooth), for every sufficiently large $X$ there are more than $X^{1-\delta}$ primes $p\le X$ such that $p-1$ is $X^\delta$-smooth. Indeed, take $x=X/5$ in that theorem; its count $x^{1-o(1)}$ exceeds $X^{1-\delta}$ eventually, and $x^\delta\le X^\delta$.

Let $\mathcal P$ be this set of primes, let $M=|\mathcal P|$, and put $k=\lfloor X^{2\delta}\rfloor$. Since $3\delta<1$, we have $k\le M$ for sufficiently large $X$. Different $k$-element subsets of $\mathcal P$ have different products by unique factorization. There are at least <a id="eq:many-prime-products"></a>$$\label{eq:many-prime-products}
 \binom Mk\ge\left(\frac Mk\right)^k
 >X^{(1-3\delta)k}$$ such products. For completeness, the binomial inequality follows by writing $\binom Mk=\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}(M-j)/(k-j)$ and observing that each factor is at least $M/k$.

For a squarefree product $m=\prod_{p\in\mathcal Q}p$ with $|\mathcal Q|=k$, multiplicativity of the totient gives $$\varphi(m)=\prod_{p\in\mathcal Q}(p-1).$$ This value is $X^\delta$-smooth and at most $X^k$. Every prime exponent in such a value is at most $k\log X/\log2$, and there are at most $X^\delta$ primes available. Hence the total number of possible values is at most <a id="eq:few-totient-values"></a>$$\label{eq:few-totient-values}
 \left(1+\frac{k\log X}{\log2}\right)^{X^\delta}
 =X^{o(k)}.$$ The last equality means that the logarithm of its left side, divided by $k\log X$, tends to zero: its numerator is $O_\delta(X^\delta\log X)$, whereas $k\asymp X^{2\delta}$.

By [eq:many-prime-products](#eq:many-prime-products), [eq:few-totient-values](#eq:few-totient-values), some $n\le X^k$ therefore satisfies $$g(n)\ge X^{(1-3\delta-o(1))k}>n^{1-\varepsilon}$$ for sufficiently large $X$. The strict final inequality follows from $3\delta+o(1)<\varepsilon$ and $1-\varepsilon>0$.

These lower bounds for $g(n)$ tend to infinity with $X$. By [lem:finite-totient-fibers](#lem:finite-totient-fibers), the resulting values $n$ cannot belong to a fixed finite set. They are therefore unbounded, which proves the required assertion for infinitely many $n$. If $\varepsilon\ge1$, the assertion follows from any smaller positive choice of $\varepsilon$. ◻

</details>

      "caption": "Main result dependencies. The auxiliary branch includes candidate mass, Type I distribution, proxy discrepancy, the elementary sieve, and the separate balanced-range bound. Arrows record the implications used in the proof; the order of choosing constants is described in the text.",
      "figure_id": "fig:proof-dependencies",
      "pdf_page": 4,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:proof-dependencies",
          "exact_quote": "Main result dependencies. The auxiliary branch includes\ncandidate mass, Type I distribution, proxy discrepancy, the elementary\nsieve, and the separate balanced-range bound. Arrows record the implications used\nin the proof; the order of choo",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,\n  box/.style={draw=blue!45!black,fill=blue!3,rounded corners=2pt,\n              align=center,text width=3.15cm,minimum height=.78cm,\n              inner sep=4pt,font=\\small},\n  link/.style={->,thick,draw=blue!55!black}]\n \\node[box] (transfer) at (0,0)\n   {Transference\\\\\\Cref{thm:transference}};\n \\node[box] (ideal) at (3.8,0)\n   {Small ideal norms\\\\\\Cref{thm:ideal}};\n \\node[box] (shift) at (1.9,-1.65)\n   {Shift cancellation\\\\\\Cref{thm:shift}};\n \\node[box] (typeii) at (6,-1.65)\n   {Type II estimate\\\\\\Cref{thm:typeii}};\n \\node[box] (sieve) at (7.6,0)\n   {Candidate, Type I,\\\\and sieve bounds\\\\\\Cref{sec:primes}};\n \\node[box] (smooth) at (11.4,-1.1)\n   {Smooth shifted primes\\\\\\Cref{thm:smooth}};\n \\node[box] (totient) at (11.4,-2.6)\n   {Large totient fibers\\\\\\Cref{thm:totient}};\n \\draw[link] (transfer)--(shift);\n \\draw[link] (ideal)--(shift);\n \\draw[link] (shift)--(typeii);\n \\draw[link] (typeii)--(smooth);\n \\draw[link] (sieve)--(smooth);\n \\draw[link] (smooth)--(totient);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Flowchart diagram illustrating dependency relationships among results using seven rectangular boxes connected by directed arrows.\nNodes:\n- \"Transference Theorem 3.5\" (top-left)\n- \"Small ideal norms Theorem 4.1\" (top-center)\n- \"Shift cancellation Theorem 5.1\" (middle-left)\n- \"Type II estimate Theorem 6.1\" (middle-center)\n- \"Candidate, Type I, and sieve bounds Section 7\" (top-right)\n- \"Smooth shifted primes Theorem 1.2\" (middle-right)\n- \"Large totient fibers Theorem 1.1\" (bottom-right)\nEdges (arrows):\n- Directed arrow from \"Transference Theorem 3.5\" to \"Shift cancellation Theorem 5.1\"\n- Directed arrow from \"Small ideal norms Theorem 4.1\" to \"Shift cancellation Theorem 5.1\"\n- Directed arrow from \"Shift cancellation Theorem 5.1\" to \"Type II estimate Theorem 6.1\"\n- Directed arrow from \"Type II estimate Theorem 6.1\" to \"Smooth shifted primes Theorem 1.2\"\n- Directed arrow from \"Candidate, Type I, and sieve bounds Section 7\" to \"Smooth shifted primes Theorem 1.2\"\n- Directed arrow from \"Smooth shifted primes Theorem 1.2\" to \"Large totient fibers Theorem 1.1\""
    },
    {
      "caption": "One possible lifespan. Solid segments denote shared activity; dashed segments denote memory. Every marked use and ghost endpoint has the same offset modulo $p$. The reciprocal prime factor is charged only at birth; $η_{t} = 1 − q_{t}$ at visit $t$.",
      "figure_id": "fig:prime-lifespan",
      "pdf_page": 18,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:prime-lifespan",
          "exact_quote": "One possible lifespan.  Solid segments denote shared\nactivity; dashed segments denote memory.  Every marked use and\nghost endpoint has the same offset modulo $p$.  The reciprocal\nprime factor is charged only at birth; $\\eta_t=1-q_t$ at visi",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.25cm,y=0.85cm,>=Stealth,\n  every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n \\draw[->,gray] (-.3,0)--(9.4,0) node[right]{visit};\n \\foreach \\x in {0,...,9}{\\draw[gray] (\\x,-.07)--(\\x,.07);}\n \\draw[very thick,dashed,blue!65!black] (0,0)--(2,0);\n \\draw[very thick,blue!65!black] (2,0)--(3,0);\n \\draw[very thick,dashed,blue!65!black] (3,0)--(6,0);\n \\draw[very thick,blue!65!black] (6,0)--(7,0);\n \\draw[very thick,dashed,blue!65!black] (7,0)--(9,0);\n \\foreach \\x in {2,3,6,7}{\\fill[blue!65!black] (\\x,0) circle (2.6pt);}\n \\foreach \\x in {0,9}{\\draw[draw=blue!65!black,fill=white,very thick]\n    (\\x,0) circle (2.6pt);}\n \\node[above] at (0,.1){ghost birth at $j$};\n \\node[above] at (2.5,.1){active};\n \\node[above] at (6.5,.1){active};\n \\node[above] at (9,.1){terminate at $i$};\n \\node[below,align=center] at (0,-.15){$-\\eta_j$\\\\$1/(pb_p)$ once};\n \\node[below,align=center] at (2,-.15){promote\\\\$1/V_g$};\n \\node[below,align=center] at (3,-.15){store\\\\$r=0$};\n \\node[below,align=center] at (6,-.15){promote\\\\$1/V_g$};\n \\node[below,align=center] at (7,-.15){store};\n \\node[below] at (9,-.15){$-\\eta_i$};\n \\node[above,align=center] at (4.5,.45){pending: $r\\gets r+\\Delta$\\\\\n                                  unmarked zero pays $q_t$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A horizontal timeline diagram showing a state evolution from left to right:\n- Left endpoint: open circle labeled above with \"ghost birth at j\", and labeled below with \"$-\\eta_j$\" and \"$1/(p b_p)\\text{ once}$\".\n- Extending from the birth endpoint is a dashed horizontal line segment labeled \"active\" above.\n- Intermediate node: solid circle labeled above with \"promote\" and \"$1/V_g$\", and below with \"store\" and \"$r = 0$\".\n- Following this node is a solid horizontal line segment labeled above with \"pending: $r \\leftarrow r + \\Delta$\" and \"unmarked zero pays $q_t$\".\n- Next intermediate node: solid circle labeled above with \"promote\" and \"$1/V_g$\", and below with \"store\".\n- Extending from this node is a dashed horizontal line segment labeled \"active\" above, terminating in a right-pointing arrow into an open circle.\n- Right endpoint: open circle labeled above with \"terminate at i\", labeled below with \"$-\\eta_i$\", and accompanied by a dashed arrow pointing right labeled \"visit\"."
    },
    {
      "caption": "Forward determinant identities for factorizations with common $e, n$. Cross-paired factors give $z = m′h$ and $z + k = mh′$; the vertical arrows record signed increments, so either sign of $k$ is allowed. The converse reconstruction, including its congruence, positivity, and coprimality conditions, is proved in the text.",
      "figure_id": "fig:determinant",
      "pdf_page": 51,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:determinant",
          "exact_quote": "Forward determinant identities for factorizations with common\n$e,n$. Cross-paired factors give $z=m'h$ and $z+k=mh'$; the vertical\narrows record signed increments, so either sign of $k$ is allowed.\nThe converse reconstruction, including its",
          "source_object": "fig:0003"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,every node/.style={font=\\small},\n  factor/.style={circle,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!3,\n                 minimum size=7mm,inner sep=2pt}]\n \\node[factor] (m) at (0,.9) {$m$};\n \\node[factor] (h) at (4,.9) {$h$};\n \\node[factor] (mp) at (0,-.9) {$m'$};\n \\node[factor] (hp) at (4,-.9) {$h'$};\n \\draw[->,gray!80!black] (m)--node[left]{$+2ek$}(mp);\n \\draw[->,gray!80!black] (h)--node[right]{$+kn$}(hp);\n \\draw[thick,blue!60!black] (m)--\n   node[pos=.26,above,sloped]{$mh'=z+k$}(hp);\n \\draw[thick,dashed,blue!60!black] (mp)--\n   node[pos=.26,below,sloped]{$m'h=z$}(h);\n \\node[align=left,anchor=west] at (6,0)\n   {$mn-2eh=1$\\\\[3pt]\n    $m'n-2eh'=1$\\\\[5pt]\n    $mh'-m'h=k\\ne0$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A diagram of four circular nodes arranged in a rectangle with connecting arrows, alongside algebraic equations:\n- Top-left circle labeled \"$m$\"\n- Bottom-left circle labeled \"$m'$\"\n- Top-right circle labeled \"$h$\"\n- Bottom-right circle labeled \"$h'$\"\n- Vertical downward solid arrow from $m$ to $m'$ labeled \"$+2ek$\"\n- Vertical downward solid arrow from $h$ to $h'$ labeled \"$+kn$\"\n- Dashed diagonal arrow from $m'$ to $h$ labeled \"$m'h = z$\"\n- Dashed diagonal arrow from $m$ to $h'$ labeled \"$mh' = z + k$\"\n- To the right of the diagram, three equations are listed:\n  $mn - 2eh = 1$\n  $m'n - 2eh' = 1$\n  $mh' - m'h = k \\ne 0$"
    }
  ],
